Problem and solution

Problem

This question is a followup of part b. In this question, we no longer fix the route in part b but find one to maximize the carrier's profit. Suppose that the carrier receives a fixed reward of r for each delivery and spends \ell, which equals the total lengths of the edges that s/he travels from pickup to delivery. In total, s/he makes a profit of r - \ell on this delivery. (We have set the cost factor of travel distance to 1, for simplicity.) Suppose that the carrier just picked up the original order and has this order only. What is his/her optimal route assuming the scooter's trunk has a capacity of 2 orders? You shall consider both the travel distance as a cost and the possible additional profit of r for picking up a new order. Because any new order has the same destination, its travel cost is ignored. Also, suppose that 0 \leq P_{e} \leq \min \{\ell_{e} / r, 1\}, where \ell_{e} is the length of edge e and P_{e} is defined in part b.

Solution

Step 1
To solve this problem, let's break it down into manageable steps and use the given information effectively. The main goal is to determine the optimal route for the carrier considering both the cost of travel (\(\ell\)) and the potential profit \(r\) from delivering an additional order, under the condition that the carrier's scooter can carry at most two orders at a time.
Step 2
First, we need to understand the constraints and given information: - **Objective**: Maximize the carrier's profit per delivery. - **Profit Formula**: Profit = \(r - \ell\). - **Capacity Constraint**: The scooter can carry up to 2 orders. - **Cost Factor**: Cost of travel is directly proportional to the length of the edge traversed (\(\ell_e\)), with the cost factor being 1 for simplicity. - **Additional Profit Condition**: For any new order, there is an additional profit of \(r\), but the travel cost for reaching the destination is ignored.
Step 3
Given that any new order's travel cost is ignored, the focus shifts entirely to minimizing the travel cost (\(\ell\)) to maximize the overall profit. The profit equation becomes crucial in deciding whether to deliver the original order or wait for an additional order.
Step 4
Next, we analyze the probability and length ratio \(P_e\). The probability \(P_e\) represents the likelihood that a new order will be available when the carrier reaches a certain location \(e\). The condition \(0 \leq P_e \leq \min \left\{ \frac{\ell_e}{r}, 1 \right\}\) indicates that the benefit of accepting a new order is limited by the ratio of the travel cost to the potential profit plus the inherent probability of finding a new order at location \(e\).
Step 5
The decision to accept a new order or not depends on whether the expected additional profit outweighs the cost of traveling to the next location and waiting for an order (if one is available). Specifically: - If \(P_e > \frac{\ell_e}{r}\): It is beneficial to travel to location \(e\) since the expected profit from potentially finding a new order exceeds the cost of travel. - If \(P_e \leq \frac{\ell_e}{r}\): It might be more profitable to skip location \(e\) if the probability of finding a new order does not justify the travel cost.
Step 6
To determine the optimal route: - Start at the origin. - Consider each location \(e\) sequentially based on their order of appearance in the graph. - Calculate the expected profit for visiting \(e\) (which includes the original order and the potential new order). - Decide whether to visit \(e\) based on the comparison between \(P_e\) and \(\frac{\ell_e}{r}\). - Optimize the sequence of visits to ensure the maximum number of orders are delivered within the scooter's capacity of 2 orders.
Step 7
The optimal route involves calculating the expected profit at each potential stop, factoring in the probability of finding a new order and the cost of travel, and making decisions that maximize the total profit while adhering to the scooter's capacity constraint. This approach requires evaluating each segment of the route individually and determining the best sequence of stops that maximizes the profit margin.
Step 8
The final answer is that the optimal route is determined by dynamically assessing each location's potential contribution to profit versus the travel cost, ensuring that the route maximizes the carrier's earnings under the given constraints. This solution process involves a combination of probabilistic analysis and cost-benefit evaluation at each decision point along the route.

Step named most at the end of each critic's reasoning (rough text search; number of critics): Step 5 (4)

Problem and solution

Problem

Let $$\begin{aligned} & A=(1+2 \sqrt{2}+3 \sqrt{3}+6 \sqrt{6})(2+6 \sqrt{2}+\sqrt{3}+3 \sqrt{6})(3+\sqrt{2}+6 \sqrt{3}+2 \sqrt{6})(6+3 \sqrt{2}+2 \sqrt{3}+\sqrt{6}) \\ & B=(1+3 \sqrt{2}+2 \sqrt{3}+6 \sqrt{6})(2+\sqrt{2}+6 \sqrt{3}+3 \sqrt{6})(3+6 \sqrt{2}+\sqrt{3}+2 \sqrt{6})(6+2 \sqrt{2}+3 \sqrt{3}+\sqrt{6}) \end{aligned}$$ Compute the value of $A / B$.

Solution

Step 1
To solve for \( \frac{A}{B} \), we first need to analyze the structure of the expressions for \( A \) and \( B \). Given: \[ A = (1 + 2\sqrt{2} + 3\sqrt{3} + 6\sqrt{6})(2 + 6\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3} + 3\sqrt{6})(3 + \sqrt{2} + 6\sqrt{3} + 2\sqrt{6})(6 + 3\sqrt{2} + 2\sqrt{3} + \sqrt{6}) \] \[ B = (1 + 3\sqrt{2} + 2\sqrt{3} + 6\sqrt{6})(2 + \sqrt{2} + 6\sqrt{3} + 3\sqrt{6})(3 + 6\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3} + 2\sqrt{6})(6 + 2\sqrt{2} + 3\sqrt{3} + \sqrt{6}) \]
Step 2
We observe that each term in \( A \) and \( B \) is a product of four factors, and the terms in \( A \) and \( B \) are similar but permuted. To simplify the problem, we can consider the symmetry and the properties of the terms.
Step 3
Let's denote the terms in \( A \) as follows: \[ A_1 = 1 + 2\sqrt{2} + 3\sqrt{3} + 6\sqrt{6} \] \[ A_2 = 2 + 6\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3} + 3\sqrt{6} \] \[ A_3 = 3 + \sqrt{2} + 6\sqrt{3} + 2\sqrt{6} \] \[ A_4 = 6 + 3\sqrt{2} + 2\sqrt{3} + \sqrt{6} \]
Step 4
And the terms in \( B \) as follows: \[ B_1 = 1 + 3\sqrt{2} + 2\sqrt{3} + 6\sqrt{6} \] \[ B_2 = 2 + \sqrt{2} + 6\sqrt{3} + 3\sqrt{6} \] \[ B_3 = 3 + 6\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3} + 2\sqrt{6} \] \[ B_4 = 6 + 2\sqrt{2} + 3\sqrt{3} + \sqrt{6} \]
Step 5
We need to compute \( \frac{A}{B} = \frac{A_1 A_2 A_3 A_4}{B_1 B_2 B_3 B_4} \). Notice that the terms in \( A \) and \( B \) are cyclic permutations of each other. Specifically, if we consider the polynomial whose roots are the terms, the product of the roots (which is the constant term of the polynomial) will be the same for both \( A \) and \( B \).
Step 6
This is because the terms are symmetric and the product of all the terms in \( A \) and \( B \) will be the same due to the symmetry and the fact that the terms are just permutations of each other. Therefore, we have: \[ \frac{A}{B} = 1 \]
Step 7
Thus, the value of \( \frac{A}{B} \) is: \[ \boxed{1} \]

Problem and solution

Problem

We know that $2021=43 \times 47$. Is there a polyhedron whose surface can be formed by gluing together 43 equal non-planar 47-gons?

Solution

Step 1
To determine if there exists a polyhedron whose surface can be formed by gluing together 43 equal non-planar 47-gons, we need to understand some basic principles about polyhedra and polygons. First, the surface of a polyhedron is composed of polygonal faces. These faces meet at edges and vertices. Second, non-planar 47-gons are polygons that cannot lie flat in a plane; they might have certain properties or shapes that make them non-planar.
Step 2
Euler's formula for any convex polyhedron (and by extension, many non-convex ones) states: \[ V - E + F = 2 \] where: - \(V\) is the number of vertices, - \(E\) is the number of edges, - \(F\) is the number of faces.
Step 3
Given that the surface can be formed by gluing together 43 equal non-planar 47-gons, each 47-gon represents one face of the polyhedron. Thus, \(F = 43\).
Step 4
For each 47-gon, every edge is shared with another 47-gon except for the edges on the boundary of the overall polyhedron. Since each 47-gon has 47 edges, initially, it might seem that there would be \(47 \times 43\) edges, but this counts every edge twice (once for each 47-gon it borders). Therefore, the total number of edges \(E\) is half of this, i.e., \(\frac{47 \times 43}{2}\).
Step 5
Each vertex is where three or more edges meet in a polyhedron. Given the complexity of the arrangement of these non-planar 47-gons, calculating \(V\) directly from this information isn't straightforward without additional geometric insight. However, we can infer that since each 47-gon contributes vertices to the polyhedron, and considering how these polygons connect, we'd need to consider how many vertices each edge is connected to across the entire structure.
Step 6
Substitute \(F = 43\) and \(E = \frac{47 \times 43}{2}\) into Euler's formula: \[ V - \frac{47 \times 43}{2} + 43 = 2 \] Solving for \(V\), we rearrange: \[ V = 2 + \frac{47 \times 43}{2} - 43 \] \[ V = 2 + \frac{47 \times 43}{2} - \frac{2 \times 43}{2} \] \[ V = 2 + \frac{43}{2}(47 - 2) \] \[ V = 2 + \frac{43}{2} \times 45 \] \[ V = 2 + 945 \] \[ V = 947 \]
Step 7
Given the calculation above, it appears theoretically possible to construct a polyhedron with 43 non-planar 47-gons as faces, provided the specific geometric constraints allow for the correct number of vertices and edges that satisfy Euler's formula. The exact configuration of vertices and edges would depend on how the 47-gons are arranged to form a closed, three-dimensional shape without gaps or overlaps. The key points are: - The number of faces \(F = 43\), - The number of edges \(E = \frac{47 \times 43}{2}\), - And, after calculations, the number of vertices \(V = 947\), which satisfies Euler's formula for a polyhedron.
Step 8
Therefore, yes, such a polyhedron can exist, given the constraints and geometrical considerations. \(\boxed{\text{Yes}}\)

Step named most at the end of each critic's reasoning (rough text search; number of critics): Step 4 (3)

Problem and solution

Problem

Sixteen wooden Cs are placed in a 4-by-4 grid, all with the same orientation, and each is to be colored either red or blue. A quadrant operation on the grid consists of choosing one of the four two-by-two subgrids of Cs found at the corners of the grid and moving each C in the subgrid to the adjacent square in the subgrid that is 90 degrees away in the clockwise direction, without changing the orientation of the C. Given that two colorings are the considered same if and only if one can be obtained from the other by a series of quadrant operations, determine the number of distinct colorings of the Cs.

Solution

Step 1
To solve the problem, we need to understand the effect of the quadrant operations on the coloring of the 4-by-4 grid. Each quadrant operation involves rotating a 2-by-2 subgrid of Cs by 90 degrees clockwise. We need to determine how many distinct colorings exist when two colorings are considered the same if one can be transformed into the other through a series of these operations.
Step 2
First, let's label the 16 positions in the 4-by-4 grid as follows: \[ \begin{array}{|c|c|c|c|} \hline 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 \\ \hline 5 & 6 & 7 & 8 \\ \hline 9 & 10 & 11 & 12 \\ \hline 13 & 14 & 15 & 16 \\ \hline \end{array} \]
Step 3
The four 2-by-2 subgrids (quadrants) are: First, top-left: positions 1, 2, 5, 6 Second, top-right: positions 3, 4, 7, 8 Third, bottom-left: positions 9, 10, 13, 14 Fourth, bottom-right: positions 11, 12, 15, 16
Step 4
A quadrant operation on any of these subgrids will permute the positions within that subgrid in a cyclic manner. For example, a quadrant operation on the top-left subgrid will map position 1 to 2, 2 to 5, 5 to 6, and 6 to 1. We need to determine the orbits of the positions under these operations.
Step 5
Notice that each quadrant operation is a 4-cycle, and the four quadrants do not interact with each other. Therefore, the positions in each quadrant form a single orbit under the operations restricted to that quadrant. Since there are four quadrants and each quadrant has 4 positions, the total number of positions is 16. The operations on each quadrant are independent, so we can consider each quadrant separately.
Step 6
For each quadrant, there are 2^4 = 16 possible colorings (since each of the 4 positions can be either red or blue). However, because the operations on each quadrant are cyclic, the number of distinct colorings for each quadrant is the number of distinct necklaces of length 4 with 2 colors, which is given by the formula for the number of distinct necklaces of length \( n \) with \( k \) colors: \[ \frac{1}{n} \sum_{d | n} \phi(d) k^{n/d} \]
Step 7
For \( n = 4 \) and \( k = 2 \), this becomes: \[ \frac{1}{4} \left( \phi(1) 2^4 + \phi(2) 2^2 + \phi(4) 2^1 \right) = \frac{1}{4} \left( 1 \cdot 16 + 1 \cdot 4 + 2 \cdot 2 \right) = \frac{1}{4} \left( 16 + 4 + 4 \right) = \frac{24}{4} = 6 \]
Step 8
So, there are 6 distinct colorings for each quadrant. Since the quadrants are independent, the total number of distinct colorings for the entire 4-by-4 grid is: \[ 6 \times 6 \times 6 \times 6 = 6^4 = 1296 \]
Step 9
Thus, the number of distinct colorings of the Cs is \(\boxed{1296}\).

Problem and solution

Problem

Find all functions $ f : \mathbb{R} \rightarrow \mathbb{R} $ such that \[ f( xf(x) + 2y) = f(x^2)+f(y)+x+y-1 \] holds for all $ x, y \in \mathbb{R}$.

Solution

Step 1
## Step 1: Initial Analysis To start solving the given functional equation, we should first try to simplify it or find patterns by substituting specific values of \( x \) and \( y \). The goal is to identify any special properties or restrictions on the function \( f \).
Step 2
## Step 2: Setting \( x = y = 0 \) Substitute \( x = y = 0 \) into the given functional equation: \[ f(0 \cdot f(0) + 2 \cdot 0) = f(0^2) + f(0) + 0 + 0 - 1 \] This simplifies to: \[ f(0) = 2f(0) - 1 \] Solving for \( f(0) \) gives us: \[ f(0) = 1 \]
Step 3
## Step 3: Setting \( y = 0 \) Now substitute \( y = 0 \) into the original equation to get a simplified version: \[ f(xf(x)) = f(x^2) + f(0) + x - 1 \] Given that we found \( f(0) = 1 \), this further simplifies to: \[ f(xf(x)) = f(x^2) + x \]
Step 4
## Step 4: Setting \( x = 0 \) Substitute \( x = 0 \) into the original equation: \[ f(0 \cdot f(0) + 2y) = f(0^2) + f(y) + 0 + y - 1 \] Given \( f(0) = 1 \), this becomes: \[ f(2y) = f(0) + f(y) + y - 1 \] Substituting \( f(0) = 1 \) yields: \[ f(2y) = f(y) + y \]
Step 5
## Step 5: Substituting \( y = xf(x) \) Using the result from Step 4 and substituting \( y = xf(x) \) into the equation \( f(2y) = f(y) + y \) gives us: \[ f(2xf(x)) = f(xf(x)) + xf(x) \] But from Step 3, we know \( f(xf(x)) = f(x^2) + x \). So, combining these insights, we aim to derive a relationship between \( f(x^2) \) and other terms.
Step 6
## Step 6: Deriving \( f(x^2) \) Relationship However, upon reevaluation, we see that the correct path involves leveraging the relationships established directly. From Step 3, we recall \( f(xf(x)) = f(x^2) + x \). To directly tie this to \( f(x) \), let's reconsider our approach focusing on the original equation's structure and the derived relationships.
Step 7
## Step 7: Revisiting the Original Equation Let's set \( y = \frac{x^2 - xf(x)}{2} \) in the original equation: \[ f\left(xf(x) + 2\left(\frac{x^2 - xf(x)}{2}\right)\right) = f(x^2) + f\left(\frac{x^2 - xf(x)}{2}\right) + x + \frac{x^2 - xf(x)}{2} - 1 \] This simplifies to an expression involving \( f(x^2) \), \( f\left(\frac{x^2 - xf(x)}{2}\right) \), and other terms, which can be used to derive a direct expression for \( f(x) \) by comparing coefficients and using previously established relationships.
Step 8
## Step 8: Simplifying and Solving for \( f(x) \) However, this approach leads to unnecessary complexity. A more straightforward method involves recognizing the functional equation's symmetry and potential for substitution that directly reveals \( f(x) \). Given the misstep in the detailed derivation process, we recognize the need to align our strategy with solving functional equations typically, focusing on substitutions that reveal the function's form.
Step 9
## Step 9: Correct Approach and Solution Re-evaluating our steps, a critical insight comes from realizing that a specific, direct substitution or recognition of the equation's symmetry might yield \( f(x) \) immediately. Given the oversight in the detailed step-by-step solving process, we acknowledge the necessity of identifying key characteristics or employing specific substitutions that directly uncover the nature of \( f(x) \), potentially involving a more elegant or straightforward manipulation of the given equation. The final answer is: \[ \boxed{f(x) = x + 1} \]

Step named most at the end of each critic's reasoning (rough text search; number of critics): Step 14 (3)

Problem and solution

Problem

The numbers $a, b, c$, in that order, form a three term arithmetic sequence (see below) and $a+b+c=60$. The numbers $a-2, b, c+3$, in that order, form a three term geometric sequence. Determine all possible values of $a, b$ and $c$. (An arithmetic sequence is a sequence in which each term after the first is obtained from the previous term by adding a constant. For example, $3,5,7$ is an arithmetic sequence with three terms. A geometric sequence is a sequence in which each term after the first is obtained from the previous term by multiplying it by a constant. For example, $3,6,12$ is a geometric sequence with three terms.) Present your answer in the form of coordinates (e.g. (1, 2, 3) for a=1, b=2, c=3).

Solution

Step 1
To solve the problem, we need to use the properties of both arithmetic and geometric sequences. Let's start by expressing the given conditions mathematically.
Step 2
First, since \(a, b, c\) form an arithmetic sequence, we can write: \[ b - a = c - b \] This simplifies to: \[ 2b = a + c \quad \text{(Equation 1)} \]
Step 3
Next, we are given that: \[ a + b + c = 60 \quad \text{(Equation 2)} \]
Step 4
Additionally, since \(a-2, b, c+3\) form a geometric sequence, we can write: \[ \frac{b}{a-2} = \frac{c+3}{b} \] This simplifies to: \[ b^2 = (a-2)(c+3) \quad \text{(Equation 3)} \]
Step 5
From Equation 1, we have: \[ c = 2b - a \] Substitute this into Equation 3: \[ b^2 = (a-2)(2b - a + 3) \] Expand and simplify: \[ b^2 = (a-2)(2b - a + 3) \] \[ b^2 = 2ab - a^2 + 3a - 4b + 2a - 6 \] \[ b^2 = 2ab - a^2 + 5a - 4b - 6 \] Rearrange terms: \[ b^2 - 2ab + a^2 - 5a + 4b + 6 = 0 \quad \text{(Equation 4)} \]
Step 6
From Equation 2: \[ c = 60 - a - b \] Substitute \(c = 60 - a - b\) into Equation 1: \[ 2b = a + (60 - a - b) \] \[ 2b = 60 - b \] \[ 3b = 60 \] \[ b = 20 \]
Step 7
Substitute \(b = 20\) into Equation 2: \[ a + 20 + c = 60 \] \[ a + c = 40 \quad \text{(Equation 5)} \]
Step 8
Substitute \(b = 20\) into Equation 1: \[ 2(20) = a + c \] \[ 40 = a + c \quad \text{(Equation 5, same as before)} \]
Step 9
Now, substitute \(b = 20\) into Equation 4: \[ 20^2 - 2a(20) + a^2 - 5a + 4(20) + 6 = 0 \] \[ 400 - 40a + a^2 - 5a + 80 + 6 = 0 \] \[ a^2 - 45a + 486 = 0 \]
Step 10
Solve the quadratic equation \(a^2 - 45a + 486 = 0\) using the quadratic formula \(a = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a}\): \[ a = \frac{45 \pm \sqrt{45^2 - 4 \cdot 1 \cdot 486}}{2 \cdot 1} \] \[ a = \frac{45 \pm \sqrt{2025 - 1944}}{2} \] \[ a = \frac{45 \pm \sqrt{81}}{2} \] \[ a = \frac{45 \pm 9}{2} \] \[ a = \frac{54}{2} = 27 \quad \text{or} \quad a = \frac{36}{2} = 18 \]
Step 11
For \(a = 27\): \[ c = 40 - 27 = 13 \]
Step 12
For \(a = 18\): \[ c = 40 - 18 = 22 \]
Step 13
Verify the solutions: First, for \(a = 27\), \(b = 20\), \(c = 13\): - Arithmetic sequence: \(27, 20, 13\) (true) - Geometric sequence: \(25, 20, 16\) (true)
Step 14
Second, for \(a = 18\), \(b = 20\), \(c = 22\): - Arithmetic sequence: \(18, 20, 22\) (true) - Geometric sequence: \(16, 20, 24\) (true)
Step 15
Thus, the possible values of \(a, b, c\) are: \[ \boxed{(27, 20, 13) \text{ and } (18, 20, 22)} \]

Problem and solution

Problem

Find the smallest positive integer $n$ such that the polynomial $(x+1)^{n}-1$ is "divisible by $x^{2}+1$ modulo 3", or more precisely, either of the following equivalent conditions holds: there exist polynomials $P, Q$ with integer coefficients such that $(x+1)^{n}-1=\left(x^{2}+1\right) P(x)+3 Q(x)$; or more conceptually, the remainder when (the polynomial) $(x+1)^{n}-1$ is divided by (the polynomial) $x^{2}+1$ is a polynomial with (integer) coefficients all divisible by 3.

Solution

Step 1
To find the smallest positive integer \( n \) such that the polynomial \((x+1)^n - 1\) is divisible by \(x^2 + 1\) modulo 3, we need to determine when the remainder of \((x+1)^n - 1\) when divided by \(x^2 + 1\) is a polynomial with all coefficients divisible by 3. First, let's consider the roots of the polynomial \(x^2 + 1\). The roots are the complex numbers \(i\) and \(-i\), where \(i\) is the imaginary unit satisfying \(i^2 = -1\). We need to check the value of \((x+1)^n - 1\) at these roots. Specifically, we need: \[ (i+1)^n - 1 \equiv 0 \pmod{3} \] and \[ (-i+1)^n - 1 \equiv 0 \pmod{3}. \]
Step 2
Let's compute \(i+1\) and \(-i+1\): \[ i+1 = 1+i \] \[ -i+1 = 1-i \]
Step 3
Next, we need to find the powers of \(1+i\) and \(1-i\) modulo 3. We will use the fact that \(i^2 \equiv -1 \pmod{3}\). First, compute \((1+i)^2\): \[ (1+i)^2 = 1 + 2i + i^2 = 1 + 2i - 1 = 2i \]
Step 4
Next, compute \((1+i)^4\): \[ (1+i)^4 = (2i)^2 = 4i^2 = 4(-1) = -4 \equiv -1 \equiv 2 \pmod{3} \]
Step 5
Now, compute \((1+i)^8\): \[ (1+i)^8 = ((1+i)^4)^2 = 2^2 = 4 \equiv 1 \pmod{3} \]
Step 6
Since \((1+i)^8 \equiv 1 \pmod{3}\), the order of \(1+i\) modulo 3 is 8. This means: \[ (1+i)^n \equiv 1 \pmod{3} \quad \text{if and only if} \quad n \equiv 0 \pmod{8} \]
Step 7
Similarly, for \(-i+1\): \[ (1-i)^2 = 1 - 2i + i^2 = 1 - 2i - 1 = -2i \equiv i \pmod{3} \]
Step 8
Next, compute \((1-i)^4\): \[ (1-i)^4 = (i)^2 = -1 \equiv 2 \pmod{3} \]
Step 9
Now, compute \((1-i)^8\): \[ (1-i)^8 = ((1-i)^4)^2 = 2^2 = 4 \equiv 1 \pmod{3} \]
Step 10
Since \((1-i)^8 \equiv 1 \pmod{3}\), the order of \(1-i\) modulo 3 is also 8. This means: \[ (1-i)^n \equiv 1 \pmod{3} \quad \text{if and only if} \quad n \equiv 0 \pmod{8} \]
Step 11
Therefore, for both \((1+i)^n - 1 \equiv 0 \pmod{3}\) and \((1-i)^n - 1 \equiv 0 \pmod{3}\) to hold simultaneously, \(n\) must be a multiple of 8. The smallest positive integer \(n\) that satisfies this condition is: \[ \boxed{8} \]
